# 3D Reconstruction Daily Papers

The project automatically fetches the latest arXiv papers for 3D reconstruction and 3D Gaussian Splatting keywords.

The subheadings represent the search keywords. For each keyword, the table keeps the most recently updated arXiv records returned by the API.

Click the repository's Watch button to receive notifications from the daily issue workflow.

Last update: 2026-11-25

## 3D reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Argos: Adapt Rich Geometric Priors for Generalizable Online Scene-Change-Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10181v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots operating in dynamic environments require reliable detection of how their surroundings change over time. Existing learning-based methods largely rely on pairwise 2D image features, which struggle under large viewpoint changes and occlusions, are sensitive to noise, and show limited generalization across domains, while explicit 3D approaches typically require costly offline optimization. We show that the implicit 3D knowledge of Geometric Foundation Models (GFMs) provides a strong basis for addressing these limitations. We introduce Argos, which adapts GFM features for joint scene change detection and 3D reconstruction. To address data scarcity and take a step toward a foundation model for scene change detection, we introduce a large-scale benchmark comprising two synthetic datasets and one real-world dataset, and train jointly across diverse datasets to improve cross-domain generalization. We further introduce Argos-SLAM, a real-time system designed for robotics, which performs online change detection and change-aware 4D mapping. Across benchmarks, our framework substantially outperforms existing baselines, with gains of up to 42.01% in change IoU and 27.91% in F1, while supporting scalable deployment in changing real-world environments.</p></details> | More details on the project website: https://www.multyxu.com/argos/ |
| **[XFreq: Geometry-Aligned Cross-View Residual Learning for Sparse 3D Ultrasound Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30869v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Three-dimensional (3D) ultrasound provides valuable volumetric context for anatomical assessment, motivating reconstruction from widely available two-dimensional (2D) slices. Sparse slices leave much of the volume unobserved, even when their acquisition poses are known. Existing methods interpolate observed intensities or incorporate learned 2D image features into 3D reconstruction, but their complementary roles in predicting smooth structure and residual details remain less explored. We introduce XFreq, a geometry-aligned framework for sparse 3D ultrasound reconstruction. It uses known slice poses and finite footprints to query 2D image features at 3D locations, complementing splatted intensities and sampling geometry. A low-frequency decoder predicts a smooth 3D field from the splat representation, while a high-frequency decoder additionally incorporates the queried 2D features to predict residual details. A learned gating module regulates the residual contribution based on geometry and cross-slice feature variation. We evaluate XFreq on an in-house fetal brain dataset and the public CETUS cardiac dataset, using slices resampled from 3D ultrasound volumes and fivefold cross-validation. Among the compared methods, XFreq achieves the highest PSNR and SSIM in most settings across both datasets, reaching 26.82 dB PSNR on CETUS with 32 input slices. Ablation studies further show the contributions of local feature querying, geometric cues, and residual prediction, with gains generally increasing as more input slices are used. Our findings highlight the value of geometry-aligned cross-view feature integration for reconstructing 3D ultrasound volumes from sparse 2D slices.</p></details> |  |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Go Far, Look Less: Active Multi-Room Reconstruction with Persistent Goals and Selective Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30802v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Active 3D reconstruction aims to allocate a sparse view budget to recover complete geometry and high-fidelity renderings. However, existing methods degrade sharply in multi-room homes: myopic next-best-view scoring gives transit no credit for distant rooms and over-polishes irreducible nearby residuals, whereas dense mappers reach far rooms only by streaming thousands of frames. We propose ROAM-GS, a training-free active multi-room reconstruction framework that couples persistent goals with selective views: each decision selects a goal and a variable-horizon prefix toward it, capturing only at its endpoint. A Surface Reconstruction Memory scores poses by projecting post-update defects and viewing directions. Persistent goals credit transit with their remaining value and are released once arrivals stop measurably improving them. Without per-scene tuning, ROAM-GS achieves state-of-the-art multi-room reconstruction while preserving single-room Replica fidelity, raising mean coverage from 74% to 93% on HSSD and from 47% to 96% (+11.7 dB PSNR) on HM3D, and matching 90-minute dense mapping using $173\times$ fewer views.</p></details> |  |
| **[BridgeMVS: Bridging Multi-View Stereo and Monodepth via Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30787v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-View Stereo (MVS) and Monocular Depth Estimation (MDE) provide complementary cues for dense 3D reconstruction. While MVS leverages explicit geometry for metric depth, it struggles in challenging regions where feature matching is unreliable. Conversely, MDE provides robust structural priors but typically yields scale-ambiguous results. Existing hybrid methods mainly exploit monocular cues in a unidirectional manner, leaving the mutual enhancement between MDE and MVS insufficiently explored. To address this limitation, we propose BridgeMVS, a unified cascaded multi-stage MVS framework that bridges monocular depth features and multi-view cost volume representations through Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion (BDF). At each stage, BDF performs dynamic mono-to-cost and cost-to-mono updates, where monocular structural features guide cost volume refinement for more robust MVS depth estimation, while multi-view geometric evidence recalibrates monocular representations to improve their metric-scale consistency. Furthermore, the fused monocular and multi-view representations are progressively propagated from coarse to fine stages and constrained by stage-wise relative-scale MDE supervision and metric-scale MVS supervision, enabling cross-branch interactions to benefit subsequent high-resolution depth estimation. In addition, BDF is designed as a plug-and-play module that can be conveniently integrated into existing cascaded MVS frameworks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BridgeMVS achieves state-of-the-art performance on the DTU, Tanks-and-Temples, and ETH3D benchmarks, while simultaneously boosting the accuracy of monocular depth estimation. Notably, BridgeMVS ranks first on both the Tanks-and-Temples Intermediate and Advanced benchmarks. The source code is available at \href{https://github.com/JianfeiJ/BridgeMVS}{https://github.com/JianfeiJ/BridgeMVS}.</p></details> | Accepted by NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[SparSelect: Training-Free Sparse Attention for Efficient 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01013v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D vision models such as VGGT have achieved remarkable progress, unifying camera estimation and dense scene reconstruction in a single pass. However, their quadratic global attention makes long image sequences expensive, while existing sparse methods may favor highly attended yet value-redundant regions. To address these limitations, we introduce SparSelect, a training-free sparse attention method combining value-aware block selection and key-guided prioritization. Our value-aware block selection integrates pooled query-key relevance with value contrast to select high-priority blocks, reducing redundancy while preserving query-relevant and distinctive content. Key-guided prioritization further refines block selection by grouping similar keys and prioritizing key features underrepresented in the initial selection. Experiments on 7Scenes and NeuralRGB-D demonstrate improved pose estimation and reconstruction quality compared with FasterVGGT, together with up to $2.27\times$ faster inference than dense VGGT. Code and evaluation scripts will be made available.</p></details> | 19 pages, including references and appendices |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Neural 3D Object Reconstruction with Small-Scale Unmanned Aerial Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12458v4)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Miniaturized Uncrewed Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) can access indoor and hard-to-reach spaces, but severe constraints on payload and autonomy have limited their use in demanding tasks such as high-quality 3D reconstruction. We introduce a novel system architecture that enables autonomous, high-fidelity 3D scanning of static objects with sub-100 gram UAVs. Our core innovation lies in a closed-loop active viewpoint selection framework specifically tailored for ultra-constrained micro-platforms, advancing beyond standard static or offline active reconstruction methods. The framework establishes a dual-reconstruction pipeline that creates a real-time (RT) feedback loop between data capture and flight control. A near-RT process uses Structure-from-Motion (SfM) to generate an instantaneous point-cloud of the object. A systematic trajectory adaptation algorithm analyzes the model quality on the fly and dynamically adapts the UAV's trajectory based on parameterized spatial partitioning to intelligently capture new images of poorly covered areas, ensuring comprehensive acquisition. For the final, high-fidelity output, a non-RT pipeline employs a Neural Radiance Fields (NeRF)-based Neural 3D Reconstruction (N3DR) approach, fusing SfM-derived camera poses with precise external location data, evaluated across both radio-based Ultra Wideband (UWB) and visual motion-capture setups, to correct sensor noise and achieve superior accuracy. We implemented and validated this architecture using Crazyflie 2.1 UAVs. Our experiments, conducted in both single- and multi-UAV configurations show that algorithmic dynamic trajectory adaptation consistently improves reconstruction quality over static flight paths. This work demonstrates a scalable and autonomous solution that unlocks the potential of miniaturized UAVs for fine-grained 3D reconstruction, a capability previously reserved for much larger platforms.</p></details> | 14 pages, 18 figures, 4 tables, 48 references |
| **[Ref2Light: From One Image to Interactive Glasses-Free 3D Characters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29678v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animating character illustrations on light-field displays demands coherent binocular parallax, real-time pose articulation, and fine-grained expression control. However, existing 2D animation and 3D reconstruction pipelines cannot combine these with rapid character switching. We present Ref2Light, a feed-forward framework that maps a single A-pose illustration and continuous pose and expression controls to display-native light-field animation, without per-character 3D reconstruction, rigging, or test-time optimization. Our key insight is to decompose light-field synthesis into static identity, view-dependent parallax, and control-driven articulation. A shared encoder caches multi-scale identity features once per character for rapid switching. A conditional decoder then synthesizes ordered angular anchors from these features via backward texture transport and direct color synthesis. A global-local control scheme deforms the anchors with head-body controls and uses the updated correspondence fields to track facial regions across views for masked local facial refinement. Finally, calibrated differentiable subpixel interlacing converts the anchors into the native panel raster, enabling end-to-end native supervision without rendering all views at full resolution. On held-out VRoid identities, Ref2Light reduces head-body LPIPS by 20.1%-55.6% over 2D and 3D baselines and lowers non-target change MAE in mouth closure by 95.6%-96.5% versus monolithic decoding, while running at 34.0-40.1 FPS for 1440x2560 native rasters on a consumer GPU. Code and demo are in the Supplementary Material.</p></details> | 25 pages, 9 figures |
| **[SuperFine3D: Efficient Global-Local Recursive Refinement for Extremely Detailed 3D Geometry Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29630v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-resolution images contain rich visual details that require fine geometric structures for faithful 3D reconstruction. However, generating extremely detailed 3D geometry from images requires substantial computational resources and training data, making high-resolution geometry generation challenging for existing state-of-the-art models. In this paper, we present SuperFine3D, an efficient framework for generating extremely detailed 3D geometry through global-local recursive refinement. SuperFine3D learns a shared refinement operator from paired coarse-to-fine representations and recursively reuses it to progressively synthesize finer geometry beyond the training resolutions, without requiring additional paired supervision at target resolutions. To make recursive refinement computationally efficient, we introduce spatial-cell attention, which restricts high-resolution interactions to local spatial neighborhoods while maintaining bounded communication across neighboring cells. We further propose spatially anchored cross-scale guidance, which leverages globally contextualized coarse geometry to guide local detail synthesis and preserve structural consistency. In addition, geometry-aware training objectives encourage the refinement process to preserve stable geometric structures while introducing fine-grained surface details. Experiments demonstrate that SuperFine3D generates geometry at a $4096^3$ decoder-grid resolution, corresponding to a sparse $256^3$ latent lattice, using a shared refinement operator trained only on $512^3$--$1024^3$ representations. Compared with a full-attention recursive baseline, SuperFine3D achieves a $9.4\times$ speedup in end-to-end inference and reduces peak GPU memory usage by $23.0\%$, while improving cross-scale structural consistency and producing cleaner surfaces with finer geometric details.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ArborNet: Volume- and skeleton-aware completion of tree quantitative structure models from incomplete point clouds using anchor-conditioned graph learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29148v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Terrestrial laser scanning (TLS) enables 3D reconstruction of trees and quantitative structure models (QSMs) for estimating woody volume and structural traits. However, occlusion in single-station or sparsely sampled TLS causes incomplete branch observations and biased volume estimates. Existing point-cloud completion methods recover plausible geometry but do not explicitly preserve branch topology, cylinder radii, or woody volume. We propose ArborNet, a volume- and skeleton-aware framework for completing tree structures in the QSM domain. Each tree is represented as a TreeQSM cylinder graph encoding cylinder geometry, branch level, parent-child topology, and branch identity. A multi-relation graph encoder propagates information across parent, child, and intra-branch connections. An anchor-conditioned decoder generates missing cylinders through terminal-branch continuation and new-branch generation. Geometry, existence, branch, radius, axis-smoothness, and volume losses encourage structural consistency. During inference, generated cylinders are selected under predicted level-wise missing-volume budgets. We evaluate ArborNet on DART-simulated TLS trees and urban TLS trees from Hong Kong, using single-station QSMs as partial inputs and multi-station QSMs as references. Under budget-constrained inference, ArborNet achieves mean total-volume relative error of 0.081% and mean level-wise volume error of 0.676% against reference QSMs, while reducing skeleton geometry error relative to evaluated point-cloud completion baselines. These errors quantify agreement with reference QSMs, not accuracy against destructively measured woody volume. Ablation results show that joint volume supervision is particularly important for volume control. These results demonstrate the potential of QSM-domain completion to recover explicit woody structure while constraining geometry and volume.</p></details> |  |
| **[Div3DAfford: Diverse 3D affordance grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28917v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D affordance grounding aims to localize 3D regions specified by an interaction query. Existing methods predict a single affordance mask, overlooking the inherent diversity of human-object interactions. We introduce diverse 3D affordance grounding, a new task that requires predicting multiple plausible regions for a given object and affordance query. We then propose Div3DAffordNet, a transformer-based set prediction model capable of generating diverse affordance regions. To address the scarcity of diverse 3D affordance data, we develop an automated pipeline leveraging 2D generative models and 3D reconstruction techniques to synthesize 3D affordances, followed by lightweight human verification (reducing the annotation time by 72X), yielding the Div3DAfford benchmark. Extensive experiments show Div3DAffordNet effectively captures multi-mask affordance regions. Our method achieves the SOTA affordance grounding performance in the multi-mask setting, reducing GED-SIM from 0.745 to 0.492, while maintaining competitive single-mask performance on the standard benchmark of 19.0 mIoU.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond the section plane: Robust 3D reconstruction of multiplex immunofluorescence exposes limitations of 2D spatial cell statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28905v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial analysis of the tumour microenvironment with multiplex immunofluorescence (mIF) typically relies on single thin tissue sections, which misrepresent the 3D tissue organisation and bias spatial cell statistics in ways that remain largely unquantified. We reconstruct 24 breast and lung tumour tissue microarray cores in 3D, each from 15-20 serial mIF sections, and compare spatial cell statistics in 3D with those from individual sections. Our pipeline combines affine pre-alignment with dense deformable refinement using RoMa v2, achieving a median target registration error of 1.79 $μ$m, well below nuclear size, which allows nuclei to be linked across sections. While cell-type proportions are preserved in 2D (Pearson corr. $r \geq 0.99$), 3D analysis reveals up to 3.8 times shorter nearest-neighbour distances, 13-19% higher neighbourhood entropy, and 2-4 times more contacts per cell. Tissue composition can thus be reliably estimated from individual sections, whereas cell-cell interactions require volumetric analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[STAC++: Plug-and-Play Adaptive Spatio-Temporal Aware Cache Compression for Streaming 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28793v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming 3D reconstruction must preserve geometry under limited memory, yet STAC's fixed-resolution spatial key-value (KV) cache grows as scenes expand. We propose STAC++, a plug-and-play, training-free framework that compresses and reuses spatial memory for long-term reconstruction in unbounded scenes. Adaptive Spatial KV Cache (ASKC) organizes KV states into an octree with adaptive resolutions across regions and layers. Response-Aware Cache Compression (RACC) guides KV merging by measuring attention-output changes, balancing storage savings with geometric preservation. Temporal Context Refinement (TCR) reuses cached intermediate features and recomputes only a short backbone suffix to refine historical predictions with later observations. Integrated into four pretrained streaming backbones, STAC++ reduces spatial memory consumption while maintaining competitive reconstruction quality on long sequences and large scenes, with further quality gains from refinement at limited computational overhead. The code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.</p></details> | 11 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables |
| **[SGR3 Model: Scene Graph Retrieval-Reasoning Model in 3D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04614v3)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D scene graphs provide a structured representation of object entities and their relationships, enabling high-level interpretation and reasoning for robots while remaining intuitively understandable to humans. Existing approaches for 3D scene graph generation typically combine scene reconstruction with graph neural networks (GNNs). However, such pipelines require multi-modal data that may not always be available, and their reliance on heuristic graph construction can constrain the prediction of relationship triplets. In this work, we introduce a Scene Graph Retrieval-Reasoning Model in 3D (SGR3 Model), a training-free framework that leverages multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for semantic scene graph generation. SGR3 Model bypasses the need for explicit 3D reconstruction. Instead, it enhances relational reasoning by incorporating semantically aligned scene graphs retrieved via a ColPali-style cross-modal framework. To improve retrieval robustness, we further introduce a weighted patch-level similarity selection mechanism that mitigates the negative impact of blurry or semantically uninformative regions. Experiments demonstrate that SGR3 Model achieves competitive performance compared to training-free baselines and on par with GNN-based expert models. Moreover, an ablation study on the retrieval module and knowledge base scale reveals that retrieved external information is explicitly integrated into the token generation process, rather than being implicitly internalized through abstraction.</p></details> | We would like to further improve the manuscript |
| **[VUOD: A Versatile Unsupervised Outlier Detection Framework for Natural, Industrial, Medical Images and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28608v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unsupervised outlier detection that automatically identifies whether visual systems involve anomalous images is a significant research topic. However, most approaches are limited to natural images because they rely on frozen and pre-trained feature extractors. So that their performance cannot be maintained in practical scenarios, especially for industrial inspection and medical imaging. In this work, we first revisit this task and then introduce a versatile unsupervised outlier detection framework to enrich the application domains. The core idea of this framework is to improve feature discriminativess via exploiting intrinsic distribution priors. Evaluated on 3 domains and 14 benchmark datasets, our proposed solution achieves state-of-the-art performance and significantly outperforms existing methods. More importantly, we show its plug-and-play property that can be integrated into diverse visual applications to improve their robustness, such as image classification and 3D reconstruction.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAR3T: Generalizable Calibration-Guided Reference-View Attention Reuse for Multi-View 3D Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28591v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward multi-view 3D reconstruction relies on global attention for cross-view aggregation, but the attention cost grows quadratically with the number of views, making inference increasingly expensive for larger inputs. Existing acceleration methods compress tokens or sparsify attention, potentially losing geometric detail or restricting cross-view aggregation while leaving redundancy among attention outputs underexploited. We observe similar cross-view attention distributions in certain layers, revealing an opportunity to reduce repeated global aggregation through output reuse. However, the layers suitable for reuse vary with the input, and reusing one layer alters the features received by later layers. Similarity at individual layers therefore cannot characterize the combined effect of multiple reuse decisions on output drift. To address these challenges, we propose CAR3T, a training-free framework combining reference-view attention output reuse with offline risk calibration and online dynamic layer selection. Calibration models cumulative reuse risk, while online selection adapts reuse to each input under a calibrated budget. CAR3T retains all input tokens and attends to the keys and values of all views, reducing the core attention computation in selected layers from quadratic to linear. Experiments on VGGT, DA3, and $π^3$ show forward speedups up to $1.41\times$ with at most 100 views and $1.75\times$ on longer sequences, with accuracy trade-offs across backbones and datasets.</p></details> |  |
| **[VG3R: Video Generation Model for Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28520v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video generation models are widely used as 3D priors for novel view synthesis and 3D scene generation. Fine-tuned for perception, generation models also excel at dense prediction such as depth estimation. However, methods that apply video generation models to 3D reconstruction either predict depth without cameras or generate geometry and estimate cameras through many iterative denoising steps, which makes them slower. In this paper, we present $\textbf{VG3R}$, which turns a pretrained video generation model into a feed-forward 3D reconstruction model. Our key observation is that such a model already captures the depth and camera motion of a video, but not in the form required for reconstruction. We introduce two components: $\textit{clip-level shift prediction}$, which corrects depth distortion caused by the unknown shift in decoded inverse depth, and a $\textit{multi-layer camera head}$, which predicts per-frame cameras parameters by combining features across transformer blocks. Using Wan2.1 as its video backbone, VG3R reconstructs a scene and estimate the camera of every frame without iterative denoising. It achieves the lowest ATE and reconstruction errors among methods built on video generation models, runs about 4 and 14 times faster than Aether and Geo4D. VG3R also demonstrates competitive depth estimation and 3D reconstruction performance compared with geometry foundation models. Project page: https://github.com/KTH-RPL/VG3R</p></details> |  |
| **[A Shared-Autonomy Construction Robotic System for Overhead Works](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09695v2)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present the ongoing development of a robotic system for overhead work such as ceiling drilling. The hardware platform comprises a mobile base with a two-stage lift, on which a bimanual torso is mounted with a custom-designed drilling end effector and RGB-D cameras. To support teleoperation in dynamic environments with limited visibility, we use Gaussian splatting for online 3D reconstruction and introduce motion parameters to model moving objects. For safe operation around dynamic obstacles, we developed a neural configuration-space barrier approach for planning and control. Initial feasibility studies demonstrate the capability of the hardware in drilling, bolting, and anchoring, and the software in safe teleoperation in a dynamic environment. Experiment videos and results are available at https://kmk9846.github.io/Construction-Robot/</p></details> | 5 pages, 8 figures, 2 tables. Presented at the ICRA 2025 Workshop on Future of Construction. v2: corrected an author name and added the project page link |
| **[From Point Clouds to Engineering Models: Benchmarking Agentic 3D Reconstruction in the Built Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28197v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can translate 3D measurements into CAD geometry, but reconstructing engineering models for the built environment demands semantic interpretation and consistent construction constraints beyond surface fitting. We formulate scene-level Scan-to-BIM (Building Information Modeling) as an agentic 3D coding task and introduce S2Bench, comprising public and proprietary scans of 128 real-world scenes across five domains, paired with expert-curated reference models spanning diverse spatial scales. A shared framework lets agents measure scans in Python, build class-labeled components in a live CAD/BIM kernel, and iteratively refine their code. We also propose parallel asynchronous collaboration, where a lead delegates versioned tasks, continues modeling while workers execute, and integrates revision-bound proposals. Evaluating five agents and a collaborative team across 1,028 reconstructions shows that scan-to-model residuals directly guide geometric repair, whereas inferring semantic classes and construction relations requires joint geometric, visual, and engineering reasoning that agents struggle to ground and verify. Consequently, the leading agent fits scan geometry closely ($96\%$ coverage within $5\,\text{cm}$) yet reaches only $63\%$ mIoU, and no agent passes all engineering checks on more than $38\%$ of scenes. Coverage and engineering conformance drop with scene scale, whereas running a smaller model as a team improves all three axes with greater coverage gains on larger scenes. The project page is available at https://tumcms.github.io/S2Bench.github.io/</p></details> |  |
| **[A Multi-Center Dataset and Benchmark for SYNTAX Segmentation of Coronary Angiograms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22139v2)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dense pixel-level classification of coronary angiography is essential for automated cardiovascular diagnostics, yet the computer vision community lacks gold-standard protocols and robust datasets to tackle this topologically complex, heavily long-tailed domain. To bridge this gap, we introduce CARDIAG: a rigorous, multi-center benchmark dataset designed to evaluate deep learning architectures on fine-grained SYNTAX segment classification. Unlike prior efforts, CARDIAG systematically prevents patient- and center-level data leakage while providing an unprecedented suite of annotations: highly imbalanced multi-class labels, explicit catheter and stenosis masks, expert uncertainty maps, temporal sequence frames, and spatial metadata. Using CARDIAG, we conduct the most extensive architectural sweep in this domain, benchmarking 24 models spanning classic CNNs, Vision Transformers (ViTs), and State-Space Models (SSMs). Moving beyond naive overlap metrics, we evaluate topological preservation (clDice), vessel diameter error, and uncertainty error-detection. Our analysis reveals an insight: modern large-kernel CNNs (particularly ConvNeXt V2 with DeepLabV3+) still outperform recent ViTs and SSMs under a shared training recipe, establishing a Dice = 0.456. By ensembling complementary models, we push the baseline to Dice = 0.479. Through leave-one-center-out cross-validation and data efficiency analyses, we explore the current limits of out-of-distribution generalization. Ultimately, CARDIAG establishes a foundation for advancing medical computer vision for cardiology across many downstream tasks, including lesion detection, temporal frame interpolation, and 3D reconstruction.</p></details> | Accepted to WACV 2027 Dataset available on Zenodo at https://zenodo.org/records/19958730, code available at https://github.com/cvlab-ai/cardiag-benchmark |
| **[VeilGS: Solving Darkness as a Veil of 2D Basis Maps over Retinex 3DGS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27719v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In a dark capture, the darkness is not uniform. How much light reaches a surface depends on where it lies relative to the few light sources, and the camera compresses what remains with a tone curve. Most low-light 3D reconstruction methods either apply one curve to every pixel of a view, so a dark object beside a lamp and a bright one in an unlit corner receive the same correction, or store the darkness in the 3D representation, where it can absorb scene detail. We present VeilGS, a 3D Gaussian Splatting method for low-light novel view synthesis that treats the degradation as a veil drawn over a clear scene. Each Gaussian carries an albedo and a shading latent instead of spherical harmonics, separating surface colour from illumination. Each view is darkened by a spatially varying gain followed by a global tone curve, and rendering without this veil gives the restored scene. A small network predicts a coarse basis for the gain from the observation, and its coefficients are solved for each view rather than learned. Training through this solve discourages the Gaussians from absorbing what the veil can explain. VeilGS needs no paired normal-light supervision and stores no per-view parameters. On Gaussian-DK, its restored renders score best on no-reference NIQE and CLIP-IQA and are rated highest in a blind user study with 55 participants. On LOM, it achieves results competitive with the state of the art.</p></details> | Project page: https://errorlogic1211.github.io/VeilGS/ |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.</p></details> |  |
| **[BladeR: Scalable, Shape-Guided 3D Reconstruction of Wind Turbine Blades from UAV Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27407v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The structural integrity of wind turbine blades is critical for safe, efficient energy production. For dense reconstruction, a tiled Gaussian Splatting framework processes 45 MP images at native resolution, isolates the blade from background, and adaptively enhances detail recovery along the leading edge. Validation against commercial and open-source baselines shows significant improvements in model completeness and detail.</p></details> | 12 pages, 13 figures. Published at VISAPP 2026 |
| **[Any3R: Feedforward 3D Reconstruction from Any Camera Type](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36150v2)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Geometry foundation models recover camera poses and dense scene geometry from multiple images using feed-forward networks, but are typically designed for pinhole cameras. This assumption is embedded in the backbone itself: positional encoding locates each patch by its pixel index and the training loss weights every pixel equally, so both treat image coordinates as if they fixed the viewing direction in 3D space, which holds only for pinhole cameras. We condition both on the dense ray field, which maps each pixel to its 3D viewing direction and represents any central camera regardless of its projection model. $\textit{Angular coordinates}$ use the viewing direction of each patch for positional encoding, and $\textit{solid-angle weighting}$ scales the point loss by the angular area covered by each pixel. Together, they allow the same encoding and supervision to be used across different camera models. $\textbf{Any3R}$ is therefore a feed-forward model that reconstructs from any mix of central cameras without lens-specific retraining. On a benchmark of eight central cameras across three datasets, where every method reconstructs with its own predicted camera poses, it is the most consistently accurate method. $\textbf{Any3R}$ enables 3D reconstruction from crowdsourced and archival image collections containing diverse camera types. Project page: https://jingong-chen.github.io/Any3R/</p></details> |  |
| **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We examine how executable symbolic representations change accuracy scaling in large language models compared with natural-language reasoning. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[PosePlaner: Denoising Any Feed-Forward Pose Predictor from Pairwise Planar Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34845v2)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multi-view Transformers (e.g., DUSt3R and VGGT) have advanced 3D reconstruction by scaling training data and model capacity from two views to many views and long sequences, subsuming the classical SfM/SLAM pipeline of feature extraction, matching, and pose estimation in a single feed-forward pass. Yet this subsumption hides the interfaces between pipeline stages---correspondences, geometry, and pose are entangled in a single forward pass, leaving supervised fine-tuning as the major path to improvement and offering no mechanism to correct predictions from geometric evidence at test time. We propose PosePlaner to close this gap by reviving the classical coupling between correspondences and pose via flow matching, treating pose refinement as a conditional generation problem that takes coarse geometric matches and initial pose as input condition and fuses the resulting pose hypotheses into a single refined estimate, providing a continuous, data-driven analog of RANSAC voting. Our method is trained efficiently on two-view data, yet serves as a periodic refiner for streaming n-view pose estimators with no retraining. Empirically, it corrects failures of feed-forward predictors, substantially improves correspondence-based solvers, and reduces accumulated drift in long streaming sequences.</p></details> | Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://robbyant-research.github.io/PosePlaner/ |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[SGAR: Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction via Sparse Geometry-Guided Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26493v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward models have been very successful in 3D reconstruction with ad hoc attention designs that relate all view patches densely. However, the cost of this dense global attention grows quadratically with the number of views, preventing the model from applying to larger-scale reconstruction settings. In this paper, we argue that most dense attention computations are unnecessary from a classical structure-from-motion perspective: 3D structures stem from geometrically matching regions in 2D. We bring this principle to learning-based feed-forward reconstruction with our Sparse Geometric Attention Reconstruction (SGAR) method. In SGAR, each patch token attends to only a small set of patch tokens from other views, chosen by a lightweight learned key selection module trained to predict cross-view 3D proximity of tokens. Thus, dense global attention is replaced by much more efficient geometric-similarity-guided sparse attention. SGAR is a general method that can be applied to different feed-forward models by fine-tuning. In our experiments, we show that SGAR is the fastest among both state-of-the-art dense and sparse methods and the most accurate sparse one. It runs up to 12-times faster than dense attention at 1,000 views with stable pose accuracy where existing sparse methods fail, and shows the best performance in reconstructing more complete geometries on the same time budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[FirstCross: Convex Single-Photon 3D Reconstruction from Sparse Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26321v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-photon lidar records the arrival time of each returning photon, enabling 3D reconstruction from few views and scarce photons. However, leading neural methods such as Transientangelo require over an hour of non-convex per-scene optimization, and their accuracy degrades as photon flux decreases. We propose FirstCross, a network-free approach that instead solves a short sequence of convex problems on a voxel grid. For a solid object, the Poisson likelihood of a ray with given signal and background rates depends on the scene solely through its first crossing, the point where the ray first enters the object. Decomposing this likelihood yields two tractable terms. The first penalizes surfaces in front of detected photons and admits a tight convex closure, namely volume rendering with a running-maximum transmittance in place of NeRF's product transmittance. The second accounts for photons that no surface explains and admits a convex upper bound once the object is assumed to be thick behind each detected surface point. Compared with Transientangelo under its own real-data protocol, FirstCross achieves lower depth error across every test scene, view count, and flux level. With three views and 100 times fewer photons than at the protocol's lowest flux, our mean object depth error stays under half of Transientangelo's with all photons. Given our detected surface points, the best classical fusion has 1.3 to 1.8 times our three-view error across flux levels. On real room-scale captures with 1% of their photons, TransientNeRF has 2.8 to 7.7 times our error. FirstCross lets few-view single-photon scanners cut exposure to 1% and reconstruct an object in about a minute on one GPU.</p></details> | Project page: https://arthur031221.github.io/firstcross/ |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[GeoGSplat: Geometry-Grounded Non-Pixel-Aligned Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36760v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward Gaussian Splatting enables efficient novel view synthesis, but pixel-aligned prediction makes the primitive count grow with image resolution and view count, leading to substantial redundancy and increased computational cost. Non-pixel-aligned methods address this limitation by predicting a fixed number of Gaussians. Without explicit ray and depth constraints, these methods yield misplaced Gaussian centers, causing rendering artifacts under large viewpoint changes. To address this issue, we introduce GeoGSplat, a geometry-grounded feed-forward model that explicitly incorporates geometric priors into the non-pixel-aligned framework. A dual-branch encoder constructs Anchors and Triplane to capture coarse 3D structure and fine-grained scene features, respectively. Subsequently, a hierarchical decoder progressively decodes dense 3D points and Gaussian primitives, enabling 3D reconstruction and novel view synthesis. GeoGSplat surpasses both state-of-the-art pixel-aligned and non-pixel-aligned Gaussian Splatting methods on DTU and NRGBD in terms of novel view synthesis and reconstruction. The advantage of our geometry-grounded paradigm further increases as target viewpoints move farther from the input cameras, demonstrating stronger generalization to large viewpoint changes. Project Page: https://comedy0913.github.io/projects/geogsplat</p></details> |  |
| **[Infer3D: Inverse Generative Modeling Enables Robust 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25061v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans can understand the 3D structure of scenes even from highly unfamiliar or extreme viewpoints. Cognitive science suggests this generalization stems from a top-down generative inference strategy. In contrast, state-of-the-art 3D reconstruction neural networks typically rely on amortized, feedforward inference. While fast, this pattern-matching approach degrades when faced with out-of-distribution (OOD) scene conditions unseen during training. Inspired by the generative nature of human vision, we propose Infer3D, an inverse generative modeling framework that recovers the latent variables of a generative model to best match the monocular input. To prevent degenerate flat outputs, we couple the generative image prior with a frozen 3D lifting network that constrains the optimization space to a valid, multi-view-consistent 3D manifold. Across a variety of controlled synthetic and real-world tasks, Infer3D demonstrates superior OOD reconstruction accuracy compared to feedforward networks trained on equal or larger datasets. Furthermore, mirroring how human perception requires more time to process confusing stimuli, we introduce an adaptive inference strategy that expends more test-time compute on ambiguous OOD examples while efficiently routing in-distribution inputs. Project website: https://amanikiruga.github.io/infer3d-site/</p></details> | 23 pages, 12 figures, 15 tables. Includes references and appendices. Project page: https://amanikiruga.github.io/infer3d-site/ |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[Micrograph B-factor estimation via Wilson statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24150v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The single-particle cryo-electron microscopy imaging process is known to cause exponential decay in the amplitude and power spectra of proteins. In this paper, we suggest a novel approach wherein the B factor is determined and corrected as a preprocessing step to the reconstruction. We show such an approach can improve reconstruction resolution.</p></details> |  |

| **[Structure-Aware Sparse-View X-ray 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.10959v4)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>X-ray, known for its ability to reveal internal structures of objects, is expected to provide richer information for 3D reconstruction than visible light. Linefomer captures internal structures of objects in 3D space by modeling the dependencies within each line segment of an X-ray. Secondly, we present a Masked Local-Global (MLG) ray sampling strategy to extract contextual and geometric information in 2D projection. Plus, we collect a larger-scale dataset X3D covering wider X-ray applications. Experiments on X3D show that SAX-NeRF surpasses previous NeRF-based methods by 12.56 and 2.49 dB on novel view synthesis and CT reconstruction. Code, models, and data are released at https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/SAX-NeRF . Our project page at https://caiyuanhao1998.github.io/project/SAX-NeRF/ shows more video and interactive results. An online Gradio demo is available on HuggingFace at https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/SAX-NeRF</p></details> | CVPR 2024; The first Transformer-based method for X-ray and CT 3D reconstruction |

| **[Layer-Matched Sparse Attention for Low-Redundancy 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23554v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>VGGT reconstructs cameras and geometry from unposed images in one pass, but its global attention grows quadratically with the number of views. Diffuse layers use shared, importance-sampled hub keys, with log-weights that make the softmax numerator and denominator unbiased. Matching layers stay exact: every aggressive thinning tested cost accuracy. At equal cost, the selected layers outperform the four shifted windows tested. | **[TetraCoder: Adaptive Tetrahedral Encoding for Neural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18267v3)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Implicit and explicit representations of geometry are central in computer graphics applications and come with their pros and cons. | **[Refin3R: Geometry-Guided Refinement for Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23051v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models predict dense point maps from multiple images in a single pass. Without explicit geometric constraints, however, their predictions are often inconsistent across views and lack fine-grained precision. | **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. | **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. | **[Pointillist Maize: 3D Reconstruction of Field-Grown Maize Using RGB UAV Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20502v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Three-dimensional reconstruction can extend field phenotyping to individual maize organs, but thin, flexible, occluding structures and plant movement complicate reconstruction. A curlicue trajectory tested in a different field in 2024 produced distinguishable individual plants without visible duplicated structures, although substantial voids remained, mainly on the soil surface. | **[SemLite-GS: Semantic-Selective Parallel Gaussian Splatting for Lightweight Building Reconstruction from UAV Oblique Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19773v1)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lightweight 3D building models support urban spatial analysis, but their production often requires dense scene reconstruction, building extraction, and mesh simplification. Third, architectural planes extracted from rendered depth, opacity, and normal maps guide signed distance field optimization, producing meshes with adaptive polygon allocation and well-defined structural boundaries. Experiments on multiple large-scale UAV datasets demonstrate that our SemLite-GS substantially reduces building reconstruction time compared with representative full-scene 3DGS methods. With comparable polygon budgets, SemLite-GS achieves superior preservation of dominant planar structures and sharp architectural transitions compared with conventional dense reconstruction followed by mesh decimation. The source code is available at https://github.com/IRIS-LAB-whu/SemLite-GS.</p></details> |  |

## structure from motion
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Screening for nonrigidity in structure from motion using projected reprojection residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30769v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse structure from motion (SfM) assumes a static scene, yet deformation may still yield plausible reconstructions. Reprojection-error summaries discard how errors are arranged across points and views. We propose a diagnostic that first removes residual components explainable by small point and camera adjustments and simple spatial trends that change over time. It then measures alignment with fixed spatial and temporal patterns. The diagnostic uses one estimated SfM reconstruction and provides screening evidence rather than a physical deformation estimate. Across four synthetic tubular families, the diagnostic detected all eight acquisitions with prescribed motion, compared with five or six for summaries of reprojection-error magnitude. Each family's cutoff was the highest static score among the other three families, so the static acquisition with the highest score also screened positive. With the same cutoffs, the diagnostic retained positive detections in 19 of 20 reconstruction variants spanning changes in appearance and reconstruction pipeline, with one numerical abstention. These findings show that the arrangement of reprojection errors across points and views can reveal inconsistencies that their magnitudes alone miss. The four families were known during development, and the transfer variants reused two of them. Within this setting, the diagnostic offers a practical way to flag a plausible reconstruction for closer inspection using only its estimated cameras, 3D points and image observations.</p></details> | 34 pages including 19 pages of supplementary material. 5 main figures and 5 supplementary figures |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. | **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Neural 3D Object Reconstruction with Small-Scale Unmanned Aerial Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12458v4)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Miniaturized Uncrewed Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) can access indoor and hard-to-reach spaces, but severe constraints on payload and autonomy have limited their use in demanding tasks such as high-quality 3D reconstruction. We introduce a novel system architecture that enables autonomous, high-fidelity 3D scanning of static objects with sub-100 gram UAVs. Our core innovation lies in a closed-loop active viewpoint selection framework specifically tailored for ultra-constrained micro-platforms, advancing beyond standard static or offline active reconstruction methods. The framework establishes a dual-reconstruction pipeline that creates a real-time (RT) feedback loop between data capture and flight control. A near-RT process uses Structure-from-Motion (SfM) to generate an instantaneous point-cloud of the object. A systematic trajectory adaptation algorithm analyzes the model quality on the fly and dynamically adapts the UAV's trajectory based on parameterized spatial partitioning to intelligently capture new images of poorly covered areas, ensuring comprehensive acquisition. | **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |

| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Code: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/xxxx |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. | **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

Variable projection (VarPro) exploits this by analytically eliminating the linear variables, yielding a reduced problem with favorable properties. Via one-time preprocessing, it builds a matrix-free Schur complement operator that evaluates costs, gradients, and Hessian-vector products of the reduced problem and integrates with standard iterative NLS solvers. We characterize the applicable problem class and common special cases admitting further analytical savings. The method extends to robust costs via iteratively reweighted least squares (IRLS): symbolic preprocessing is reused across reweightings, so each IRLS iteration needs only a numerical refactorization. On 42 benchmark datasets spanning pose-graph optimization, range-aided SLAM, sensor network localization, and structure from motion, SPARSER is fastest on 41 on CPU and 35 on GPU, and is the only method to solve all. ## surface reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Neural Field Thermal Tomography: A Differentiable Physics Framework for Non-Destructive Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11045v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse problems for stiff parabolic partial differential equations (PDEs), such as the inverse heat conduction problem (IHCP), are severely ill-posed: the forward map rapidly damps high-frequency interior structure before it reaches the boundary. Soft-constrained physics-informed neural networks (PINNs), which embed the PDE as a residual penalty, decouple the diffusivity from the surface data in their standard two-network inverse formulation, and the evaluated variants fit boundary measurements while leaving the interior field essentially untouched. We propose Neural Field Thermal Tomography (NeFTY), a hard-constrained neural field framework for label-free three-dimensional inverse heat conduction. NeFTY represents the unknown diffusivity as a continuous coordinate-based neural network, and at every optimization step passes the candidate field through a differentiable implicit-Euler heat solver with harmonic-mean interface flux, so that the simulated temperature satisfies the discretized heat equation up to the solver tolerance. Adjoint gradients propagate the surface reconstruction error back to the network weights at solver-level memory cost, making test-time inversion tractable on a single GPU. Across synthetic 3D benchmarks, NeFTY outperforms soft-constrained PINN variants, a voxel-grid baseline, and classical L-BFGS voxel inversion through the same solver on label-free volumetric recovery, and it transfers to real thermography data, surpassing classical signal-processing baselines in both defect segmentation and depth estimation. | **[Occupancy Planar Patch: Incremental Probabilistic Bounded-Plane Reconstruction from LiDAR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28403v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online surface reconstruction from LiDAR scans requires observation models that respect the sensor's physical measurement geometry. | **[PAWS: A Parametric Model of Canine Pose and Shape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27720v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce PAWS, a parametric model of canine shape and pose, learned from 3D scans of real dogs. | **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. | **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search.
