Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |

## Gaussian density control
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |

Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. | **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. ## Gaussian uncertainty
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[Bayesian Bias Learning-Enabled Probabilistic Voltage Estimation with Unknown Uncertainty Modeling of DERs in Distribution Grids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27866v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>One key challenge in distribution system state es- timation is unknown input statistics from the growing inte- gration of distributed energy resources (DERs), which may largely deviate from Gaussian uncertainty. Existing model-based estimators often rely on the assumption that all inputs follow known Gaussian distributions, while data-driven methods suffer from overfitting and failure in out-of-distribution scenarios. This paper proposes a Bayesian bias learning (BBL)-enabled probabilistic voltage estimation method in distribution systems with unknown DER generation statistics. The proposed learning- enabled method consists of two stages: probabilistic uncertainty inference and computationally efficient voltage estimation. First, a physics-constrained probabilistic density inference network is developed to learn Gaussian mixture representations of DER generation from historical data. Then, the resulting probabilistic representations, treated as pseudo-measurements, are integrated into a BBL-enabled voltage estimator via a decomposition- and-parallelization mechanism. Furthermore, utilizing a linear analytical solution, the proposed BBL-enabled voltage estimator efficiently captures linearization-induced biases and attains ac- curate probabilistic estimation. By integrating learning-enabled probabilistic uncertainty modeling into the parallel estimation structure, the proposed method achieves uncertainty-aware scal- able voltage estimation. Case studies on two unbalanced IEEE distribution networks show that the proposed estimator achieves significantly higher accuracy and computational efficiency under unknown uncertainty, outperforming recent data-driven and model-based methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.</p></details> |  |
| **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. | **[Random Cut Forests, Games and Diffusion over Approximately Nested Anisotropic Local Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19548v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Just as the data in a dynamic stream changes, questions asked of the stream also change in intent: what to hold constant, which counterfactuals to consider, and so forth. Energy-based learning has been celebrated for its uniform approach to losses and estimation, but interpretability remains challenging. Tree-based methods, on the other hand, offer high interpretability but often rely on bespoke, disjointed estimators. Neither paradigm readily supports changing questions on streaming data. In this paper we take a step towards combining energy, interpretability, and streaming. Random cut forests retain an evolving geometry of the data. We study this geometry as the basis for diffusion dynamics, prediction, explanation, and games. We show that several existing scores are escape probabilities of random walks on the trees. The induced affinity is realized exactly by a sparse electrical network on each tree, giving a single energy calculus, a Hamiltonian, maintained along one path per update. This connects an estimate, its supporting evidence, and the response to intervention through the same boxes, gaps, masses, and paths. Different questions correspond to different clamp decisions on the energy. Fixing observed values gives harmonic prediction with Gaussian uncertainty. Fixing a partition gives normalized cuts whose cost is attributed exactly to oriented faces. Releasing clamps gives coalition games, in which collusive displacement measures the field response. With noise, the same energy gives diffusion dynamics whose relaxation rates are read from the boxes. Axis-parallel cuts localize at a rate set by affine rather than ambient dimension, and this control passes to the averaged energy. Across a forest the boxes are only approximately nested; common reference observations align the trees. A rotating star with added noise illustrates the measurements.</p></details> |  |

Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In SGAR, each patch token attends to only a small set of patch tokens from other views, chosen by a lightweight learned key selection module trained to predict cross-view 3D proximity of tokens. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. ## feed-forward Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[WildAnySplat: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31023v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present WildAnySplat, a feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that enables robust novel-view synthesis from in-the-wild multi-view images. Unlike controlled captures, real-world images often contain multi-view inconsistencies caused by transient occluders, illumination changes, and camera ISP, which degrade the quality of existing feed-forward reconstruction methods. To address this challenge, we introduce a student-teacher framework that learns transient-robust and appearance-consistent 3D representations. A teacher network operates on clean images, while a student processes the corresponding in-the-wild inputs. The teacher provides clean supervision to guide the student toward a consistent canonical representation. Since paired clean and in-the-wild data are scarce, we use a hybrid data strategy, combining real paired captures, synthetic augmentations, and pseudo-clean labels generated from optimization-based reconstructions, bridging the domain gap between synthetic and real-world data. We adopt a transformer architecture with dedicated DPT heads for camera pose, depth, and Gaussian prediction. We additionally predict per-view transient masks to reweight photometric supervision and suppress unreliable Gaussians. To account for cross-view appearance variation, we incorporate a bilateral grid module that models per-image color transformations and enables recovery of a canonical radiance representation. Experiments on challenging in-the-wild benchmarks show that WildAnySplat improves robustness to transient occlusions and appearance variation while retaining the fast inference of feed-forward Gaussian Splatting.</p></details> | Accepted to SIGGRAPH Asia 2026 (Conference Papers). Project page: https://wildanysplat.github.io/ ; Supplementary material: https://wildanysplat.github.io/wildanysplat_supp.pdf |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |

The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. ## pose-free 3D reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.</p></details> |  |
| **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We examine how executable symbolic representations change accuracy scaling in large language models compared with natural-language reasoning. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |

We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. | **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |

| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |

| **[UniWorld-View: Large-Baseline View Synthesis via Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04701v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Novel view synthesis from casually captured monocular images and videos offers a practical route to immersive content creation without dedicated multi-view capture. This strategy produces dense, synchronized multi-view videos that provide additional supervision for dynamic Gaussian Splatting, enabling more complete 4D generation from monocular observations. | **[SemGuard: Semantics-Decoupled Watermarking for Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28636v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gaussian Splatting provides an efficient representation for high-fidelity rendering of static and dynamic 3D scenes, motivating the copyright protection of Gaussian assets. In CLIP-guided Gaussian watermarking, a universal message decoder pretrained on textual watermark representations is subsequently applied to visual features containing both watermark information and scene-dependent semantics. However, text-only pretraining does not explicitly account for the semantic interference encountered during watermark extraction. To address this challenge, we propose SemGuard, a simple yet effective semantics-decoupled watermarking framework that learns to suppress scene-dependent semantic interference during universal message decoder pretraining. Specifically, SemGuard combines message-related CLIP text features with message-independent visual features extracted from unwatermarked scene images, constructing training representations with controllable semantic interference relevant to the target Gaussian assets. A progressive semantic interference curriculum gradually increases the contribution of visual features, allowing the decoder to first learn reliable message representations and then adapt to increasingly strong scene-dependent semantic interference. After pretraining for a given message length and semantic image pool, the decoder is frozen and reused across multiple Gaussian assets whose visual semantics are covered by the pretraining data. Experiments on static and dynamic Gaussian Splatting assets demonstrate improved rendering fidelity alongside reliable watermark extraction, while evaluations on static assets further show enhanced robustness against image-space distortions.</p></details> | 10 pages, 3 figures. Submitted to IEEE Transactions on Multimedia (under review) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. | **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |
| **[CueSplat: Learning to Schedule Sparse Deformation for Dynamic Gaussian Splatting on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22095v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deformation-based 4D Gaussian splatting (4DGS) enables dynamic scene rendering. However, evaluating deformation networks for all Gaussians at every frame is compute-intensive on edge devices. We find that Gaussian trajectories are often locally predictable, yet prediction reliability varies across Gaussians and time. Thus, we propose CueSplat, a post-training framework that exploits local temporal regularity while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen and retaining its Gaussian set. Specifically, CueSplat predicts Gaussian attributes from recent history and uses a lightweight learned router to select exact deformation updates within a scene-specific budget calibrated offline. Selection accounts for prediction reliability and rendering contribution. Selected indices are reused across nearby frames to reduce routing overhead, while selected Gaussians still receive exact updates each frame. To control accumulated prediction error, we use drift signals to schedule three consecutive full-deformation frames that rebuild the prediction history. Evaluated across four mainstream 4DGS algorithms, CueSplat achieves up to $6.74\times$ and $8.15\times$ speedups on Jetson AGX Orin and Orin NX, respectively, with a negligible quality drop (at most $0.16$ dB in dataset-average PSNR).</p></details> | 10 pages, 6 figures. Main paper only |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |

## Gaussian Splatting SLAM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[D4GS: Efficient Gaussian Splatting SLAM with Depth Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36853v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Achieving high-quality reconstruction and responsive camera tracking remains challenging for monocular RGB Gaussian splatting SLAM under a limited optimization budget. We present an online system that addresses this challenge by combining learned geometric priors with explicit geometric camera tracking, which efficiently performs camera tracking while maintaining the map to keep the predicted depth scale consistent. A frozen depth model serves as a virtual RGB-D sensor, providing depth estimation from monocular video. An explicit geometric solver then estimates camera pose through feature reprojection, and a streaming Gaussian mapper integrates the resulting observations into a dense scene representation. Extensive experiments on indoor and outdoor datasets show that our method significantly improves reconstruction quality over existing approaches, achieving over 10 FPS on a single RTX 4090 and delivering almost twice the throughput of the evaluated fastest prior SLAM systems on the 7-Scenes dataset.</p></details> | 11 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[CoSphere-SLAM: Multi-Agent Panoramic Gaussian Splatting SLAM with Spherical Loop Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22235v2)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dense collaborative mapping is essential for search and rescue, building inspection, and warehouse automation, where panoramic cameras capture the entire surroundings in a single frame. Unlike pinhole agents that share views only at similar headings, panoramic agents observe overlapping scenes at any 3D orientation, yet searching full rotations also produces mutually consistent false loop closures across distinct symmetric rooms. Moreover, continuous monocular scale drift splits rigidly merged maps at revisited surfaces, while depth noise at short baselines distorts loop translation directions. To address these challenges, we propose CoSphere-SLAM, the first multi-agent Gaussian splatting SLAM framework for panoramic cameras, whose spherical loop closure aligns spherical features over $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ via fast Fourier correlation across arbitrary orientations and rejects symmetric false closures through global $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ consensus. Building on these closures, directional global optimization solves a per-keyframe $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ pose graph with log-scale regularization to correct scale drift while separating optical-flow translation directions from depth-based magnitudes. Finally, omnidirectional Gaussian fusion transforms each Gaussian with its keyframe similarity and renders Gaussians at their maximum ray response to jointly refine poses and maps. Experiments on indoor and cross-session multi-agent panoramic benchmarks demonstrate that CoSphere-SLAM achieves state-of-the-art trajectory accuracy and rendering quality among single-agent and multi-agent methods. Project website: https://cosphere-slam.github.io.</p></details> | 9 pages, 4 figures, 7 tables. Project website: https://cosphere-slam.github.io |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |

## relightable Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| **[NNS-GS: Non-Local Neural Scattering for Relightable Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30351v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Creating relightable 3D assets of translucent objects is challenging because changing the illumination at one surface location can affect the appearance elsewhere through subsurface scattering. To reduce component mixing and support controllable scattering appearance, anchor-based progressive training establishes the diffuse-scattering base before introducing specular reflection and supervises the components separately. Under calibrated point lighting, NNS-GS improves mean PSNR over existing methods by 2.47 dB and 0.81 dB on the synthetic and real translucent scenes, respectively. Additional synthetic comparisons further show that NNS-GS better matches the reference when adjusting scattering attenuation.</p></details> |  |
